LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1:** Analyzing the spatial contradiction between evidences and testimonies, we notice that Testimony 3 states Ms. Crane was murdered, and with her dying breath, she managed to retaliate. However, the Autopsy Report (Evidence 11) indicates that the head injury was sustained post-mortem, which means the retaliation, as described, is not possible if the injury causing death was not the head injury but rather the stab wounds to the left chest, also inflicted post-mortem.

**Step 2:** Checking if any spatial propositions apply to this contradiction, we find Prop 4 relevant: If a person is in a location and a weapon is used at that location, then the person may have been injured by the weapon. This proposition can help understand the sequence of events but does not directly address the contradiction between the testimony and the evidence regarding the timing of the injuries and the possibility of retaliation.

**Step 3:** Since Prop 4 does not directly reveal the contradiction but rather informs about potential injuries, we look for a more applicable proposition. Prop 7 (If a person is hit from behind, then the person will fall forward) does not directly apply to the contradiction at hand. The critical issue here is the timing of the injuries (post-mortem) versus the testimony of retaliation, which implies an action taken by the victim before death.

**Step 4:** The spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction involves understanding the sequence of events as described by the testimonies and the physical evidence. The key contradiction lies in the implication of Testimony 3 (retaliation with dying breath) versus the evidence (post-mortem injuries), suggesting that the retaliation, as described, could not have occurred given the nature of the injuries.

**Step 5:** Given the analysis, the contradiction is between the implication of action after being mortally wounded (as per Testimony 3) and the medical evidence indicating the injuries were inflicted post-mortem (Evidence 11). Thus, the evidence and testimony that contradict each other are Evidence 11 (Autopsy Report) and Testimony 3.

**Step 4:** The spatial reasoning here involves understanding the relationship between the location of the knife, the presence of fingerprints, and the actions implied by these spatial relationships. The contradiction arises from the interpretation of these spatial clues in the context of premeditation versus involvement in the crime.

**Step 5:** Given the analysis, the contradiction is between Evidence 15 (the knife with Engarde's fingerprints) and Testimony 5 (claiming the defendant bought the knife for the crime, implying premeditation). LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1:** Analyzing the spatial contradiction between evidences and testimonies, we notice a potential discrepancy between Evidence 10 (Security Camera Footage) and Testimony 3 (Lang's statement about the president's descent). The security footage shows President Wang and Judge Gavèlle arriving on the roof but does not capture the president leaving via the elevator. Meanwhile, Lang testifies that there's no sign of the president getting on the elevator at all after Judge Gavèlle came back down alone.

**Step 2:** Checking if any spatial propositions apply to this contradiction, we find that Proposition 1 (If a person is in a room and there is a wall between the person and another location, then the person cannot directly see the other location) does not directly apply because it discusses visibility, not movement or the use of elevators. However, Proposition 4 (If a door is locked, then a person cannot pass through the door) and Proposition 5 (If a person is in front of a door, then the person can potentially pass through the door) might seem relevant but do not directly address the issue of elevator usage or the absence of the president's descent.

**Step 3:** Upon closer inspection, Proposition 7 (If a person is inside a location and the location is locked, then the person cannot leave the location) does not apply directly because there's no mention of the roof being locked. However, the concept of being unable to leave a location without using a specific exit (in this case, the elevator) is akin to the situation described. Yet, it's Proposition 11 (If a person is in a room and there is a door to another room, then the person can potentially move to the other room) that hints at the idea that if there's a means of moving between areas (like an elevator), one would expect to see evidence of its use if it were utilized for exiting.

**Step 4:** The spatial reasoning used here involves understanding the layout and the means of movement within a building. If the president went up to the roof with Judge Gavèlle and she came back down alone, and there's no footage of the president using the elevator to descend, it implies the president might not have used the elevator to leave the roof, contradicting the expectation that one would use the elevator to move between floors if it's the primary means of vertical movement.

**Step 5:** Given the analysis, the contradiction lies between the expectation of seeing the president use the elevator to leave (based on typical usage of elevators for floor transition) and the lack of evidence showing this. Thus, the evidence and testimony that contradict each other involve the security footage (Evidence 10) and Lang's testimony about the president not being seen to leave via the elevator (Testimony 3).
